        "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 DDR reflects monotonic NLL compression under recursive generation (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}).\nWe hypothesize that shape stability arises in part from architectural constraints and distributional geometry, though it is not trivially predictable: tail\\_decay lacks an obvious architectural bound yet remains near-stationary ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$); entropy\\_max is bounded by $\\log|V|{\\approx}10.8$ nats ($|V|{=}50{,}257$), but observed values fall far below this ceiling---the bound constrains range without explaining within-bound stability; and entropy\\_std---also NLL-derived---drifts substantially ($d{=}0.46$), showing NLL derivation alone does not confer stationarity.\nANOVA confirms depth explains ${>}48{\\times}$ more variance in location than shape features (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to model collapse.}\n\\looseness=-1 Model collapse erodes tail coverage via weight updates---and shape features \\emph{do} change under retraining \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}---whereas DDR characterizes fixed-parameter inference where shape remains stable.\nShape stability is thus specific to the fixed-parameter regime, not an a priori consequence of architecture; DDR complements concurrent iterative-generation studies (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature sensitivity.}\n\\looseness=-1 Higher sampling temperature ($\\tau > 0.7$) flattens token probability distributions, narrowing NLL variance and diluting the location shift that drifting features capture---the mechanism through which DDR signal degrades at elevated temperatures (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nThe DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across all tested temperatures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), but signal strength is temperature-dependent, bounding current applicability to moderate sampling.\n\n\\paragraph{Error structure.}\n\\looseness=-1 The gen-1$\\to$gen-2+ boundary is the primary failure point (error rate 48.4\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}): gen-1 texts retain sufficient diversity to overlap with gen-0; deeper generations show monotonically decreasing error ($\\leq$29.2\\%), so DDR-based classifiers are most reliable for heavily recycled content.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and applications.}\n\\looseness=-1 The DDR partition is robust across decoding strategies, temperatures, and reference models (Appendices~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}--\\ref{app:cross-ref}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), but QLoRA recovery to F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (within-model) confirms the signal persists; cross-generator instruct evaluation remains future work.\nCross-domain transfer retains F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}); a 9-generator evaluation confirms generalization beyond the original pool (Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}).\nDDR enables corpus contamination detection and synthetic data pipeline auditing; depth-aware filtering does not improve perplexity (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}), confirming DDR identifies \\emph{provenance} rather than \\emph{fluency degradation}.\nFiner-grained 5-class classification yields F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50 (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), suggesting a resolution limit of three depth classes.\n",
        "numLines": 26,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 26
      }

        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1 An estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \\cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \\emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nBase models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.\nWe find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \\emph{location} drifts with depth while \\emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.\n\n\\looseness=-1 Distributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth profiling} (coarse ordinal classification into gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy\\footnote{Throughout, ``entropy'' denotes per-token NLL under the reference model (Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \\S\\ref{sec:features}.}, lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ more stable (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.46$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.\nThe stationary shape component complements location drift for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \\textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\\rho| > 0.59$). \\textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (red for drifting, blue for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}\n\\end{figure*}\nWe focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:framework} summarizes the pipeline: recursive generation chains (\\S\\ref{sec:generation}), feature extraction with DDR partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:features}), and leave-one-generator-out evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA \\cite{qlora} (Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation) classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into two primary response zones---drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) vs.\\ near-stationary ($d < 0.10$)---plus one intermediate feature ($d{=}0.46$). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges} \\cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \\emph{what is approximately preserved}, an empirical regularity with analytical utility for depth classification without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B; exploratory evidence extends to 14B, Appendix~\\ref{app:9gen-scale}), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n    \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, model scale, domain transfer, and perturbation robustness maps the operational envelope of depth profiling.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

{
  "parentUuid": "711d3632-85bd-4398-bb51-15cd349799dc",

  "attachment": {

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "% Abstract — Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains\n\\begin{abstract}\nAs large language model (LLM) outputs re-enter training corpora and seed further generation, understanding how recursive generation reshapes text distributions is critical for data provenance and corpus curation---yet existing detectors answer only the binary human-or-machine question, ignoring \\emph{generation depth}: we introduce \\emph{depth profiling} to address this gap.\nWe decompose distributional drift under recursive generation into location and shape components, revealing an asymmetry we term \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR): across eight base-model generators from five families, negative log-likelihood (NLL) location features drift monotonically with depth (effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) while three shape features remain near-stationary ($d < 0.10$), with entropy std intermediate ($d{=}0.46$)---a ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ effect-size gap.\nPrior work documents \\emph{what converges}; the central finding here is \\emph{what is approximately preserved}: shape stability complements location drift for ordinal depth grading without per-generator calibration, achieving leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+), cross-model binary F1\\,=\\,0.830, and 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.762 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation (gen-0 seeds shared across generators; \\S\\ref{sec:generation}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR, bounding current applicability to base models. We release our code and data at\\footnote{\\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026-2/gen-depth-fingerprint}}.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 8,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex"
  },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Recursive generation produces a decomposable depth response---location features drift monotonically while shape features remain approximately preserved---enabling cross-generator depth classification (F1\\,=\\,0.694--0.762) under moderate sampling temperatures ($\\tau \\leq 0.7$).\nCross-model chains confirm generalization across five families (F1\\,=\\,0.830; ANOVA: depth explains ${>}48{\\times}$ more location-feature variance than shape-feature variance, generator identity ${<}1$\\%).\nThe decomposition is robust across decoding strategies, temperatures, prompt types, and reference models, with the DDR partition independently recovered by every held-out generator.\nQLoRA recovery (F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91, within-model) suggests tractable extension to instruct models; DDR connects depth profiling to corpus auditing and model collapse monitoring.\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "8e39775d-3022-4306-a4d1-3adb3c5bb538",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\vspace{-1mm}\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{table}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (\\S\\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals entropy mean, TTR, and hapax ratio drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.0cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{Measures} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{entropy\\_mean} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)} & Ref.\\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_std} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_max} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_min} \\textit{\\scriptsize(NLL)}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{tail\\_decay}   & Ref.\\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\\\\n\\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig2_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Depth profiling pipeline. (a)~Human seeds are iteratively fed through generator $G$ to produce chains of increasing depth. (b)~Seven features extracted from NLL distributions group into three DDR zones: drifting (red), intermediate (purple), near-stationary (blue). (c)~LOGO evaluation holds out one generator for testing.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\n\\looseness=-1 We construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\n\\looseness=-1 These span a 13$\\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B) across five architectures; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8) is a limitation mitigated by LOFO (F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same seed pool serves as gen-0 for all generators, controlling for input content; in LOGO evaluation this creates a potential train--test overlap for gen-0, addressed by our binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class LOGO yields macro F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50, with depths 2--4 near-indistinguishable (gen-2/gen-3 per-class F1\\,$<$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.\n\nWe use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$10.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition (LOFO confirms both feature groups contribute independently; Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 We extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), covering the NLL distribution's location, spread, extremes, and tail structure, plus two reference-free surface diversity measures; six additional NLL statistics were rejected for redundancy ($|r| > 0.65$, $\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\n\\looseness=-1 A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nCross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\n\\looseness=-1 We evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\n\\looseness=-1 We use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); hyperparameter sensitivity (max\\_depth sweep) shows F1 variation $\\leq$2.0\\,pp, confirming discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",
        "numLines": 97,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 97
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/method.tex"
  },

```text
1	\vspace{-1mm}
2	\section{Conclusion}
3	\label{sec:conclusion}
4	
5	\looseness=-1 Recursive generation produces a decomposable depth response---location features drift monotonically while shape features remain approximately preserved---enabling cross-generator depth classification (F1\,=\,0.694--0.762) under moderate sampling temperatures ($\tau \leq 0.7$).
6	Cross-model chains confirm generalization across five families (F1\,=\,0.830; ANOVA: depth explains ${>}48{\times}$ more location-feature variance than shape-feature variance, generator identity contributes ${\approx}2$\%).
7	The decomposition is robust across decoding strategies, temperatures, prompt types, and reference models, with the DDR partition independently recovered by every held-out generator.
8	QLoRA recovery (F1\,=\,0.89--0.91, within-model) suggests tractable extension to instruct models; DDR connects depth profiling to corpus auditing and model collapse monitoring.
9	
```

I should sync discussion.tex from artifacts to remove the inline figure and see if that gets the Conclusion back within bounds.

case），但正文从头到尾没有一句话指向它们。这种\"孤儿附录内容\"对 reviewer 来说等于不存在 —— 既浪费篇幅，也让附录显得拼凑。\n\nACL 政策也明确：附录是 supplementary 性质的延伸，必须由正文驱动读者去看。完全没被引用的附录章节会被视为低质量补充材料。\n\n---\n\n自检要求：\n\n1. 逐项盘点附录\n   把附录里的每一个 section / subsection / table / figure / algorithm / proof / prompt 都列出来。常见类型：\n   - Appendix A: Implementation details / Training details\n   - Appendix B: Additional results / Ablations\n   - Appendix C: Hyperparameter search\n   - Appendix D: Prompt templates\n   - Appendix E: Proofs\n   - Appendix F: Failure cases / Error analysis / Qualitative examples\n   - Appendix G: Dataset construction details\n\n2. 逐项验证引用\n   对每一项，在正文里搜：是否有至少一句话指向它？引用形式应该是：\n   - \"... (see Appendix A for full hyperparameters)\"\n   - \"... we defer the proof to Appendix B\"\n   - \"... Table 5 in Appendix C reports the breakdown\"\n   - \"... Figure 7 (Appendix D) visualizes ...\"\n\n   不合格的\"引用\"：\n   ❌ 只在 paper 末尾甩一句 \"See appendix for more details\"（没指向具体 section）\n   ❌ 在 Limitations 或 Conclusion 才提一次（应该在内容相关的章节就引出）\n   ❌ 引用了但没说明为什么读者要去看（\"see Appendix B\" 没下文）\n\n3. 修复策略（按优先级）\n   - 优选：在最相关的正文章节加一句自然的引用。例如训练细节 → 在 Experimental Setup 末尾：\"For full training details including learning rate schedule and gradient clipping, see Appendix A.\"\n   - 次选：如果实在找不到合适位置插入引用，且该附录内容对核心 claim 不必要，直接删除附录内容（不要保留无引用的\"装饰性附录\"）\n   - 不要做：在某个章节最后硬塞一行 \"See Appendix X.\" 当作补丁——这不算合理引用\n\n4. 引用质量原则\n   每条引用应该让 reviewer 立刻明白：\n   - 为什么他要去翻那里（解决什么疑问 / 验证什么 claim）\n   - 能在那里找到什么（具体是表、图、proof 还是定性例子）\n\n---\n\n完成后请汇报：\n\n(a) 附录有多少个 section / subsection？分别是什么内容？\n(b) 哪些原本没有正文引用？现在加在了哪个章节哪一句话？\n(c) 是否有附录内容被你判定为\"无必要\"而删除？删掉了哪些？"

[飞书用户 用户]: 📑 论文自检任务：附录内容引用完整性检查

请检查论文附录（Appendix）的所有内容是否在正文里都有准确、合理的引用。

AI 写作的高频问题：附录里塞了一堆补充材料（额外实验、超参表、prompt 模板、proof、failure case），但正文从头到尾没有一句话指向它们。这种"孤儿附录内容"对 reviewer 来说等于不存在 —— 既浪费篇幅，也让附录显得拼凑。

ACL 政策也明确：附录是 supplementary 性质的延伸，必须由正文驱动读者去看。完全没被引用的附录章节会被视为低质量补充材料。

---

自检要求：

1. 逐项盘点附录
   把附录里的每一个 section / subsection / table / figure / algorithm / proof / prompt 都列出来。常见类型：
   - Appendix A: Implementation details / Training details
   - Appendix B: Additional results / Ablations
   - Appendix C: Hyperparameter search
   - Appendix D: Prompt templates
   - Appendix E: Proofs
   - Appendix F: Failure cases / Error analysis / Qualitative examples
   - Appendix G: Dataset construction details

2. 逐项验证引用
   对每一项，在正文里搜：是否有至少一句话指向它？引用形式应该是：
   - "... (see Appendix A for full hyperparameters)"
   - "... we defer the proof to Appendix B"
   - "... Table 5 in Appendix C reports the breakdown"
   - "... Figure 7 (Appendix D) visualizes ..."

   不合格的"引用"：
   ❌ 只在 paper 末尾甩一句 "See appendix for more details"（没指向具体 section）
   ❌ 在 Limitations 或 Conclusion 才提一次（应该在内容相关的章节就引出）
   ❌ 引用了但没说明为什么读者要去看（"see Appendix B" 没下文）

3. 修复策略（按优先级）
   - 优选：在最相关的正文章节加一句自然的引用。例如训练细节 → 在 Experimental Setup 末尾："For full training details including learning rate schedule and gradient clipping, see Appendix A."
   - 次选：如果实在找不到合适位置插入引用，且该附录内容对核心 claim 不必要，直接删除附录内容（不要保留无引用的"装饰性附录"）
   - 不要做：在某个章节最后硬塞一行 "See Appendix X." 当作补丁——这不算合理引用

4. 引用质量原则
   每条引用应该让 reviewer 立刻明白：
   - 为什么他要去翻那里（解决什么疑问 / 验证什么 claim）
   - 能在那里找到什么（具体是表、图、proof 还是定性例子）

---

完成后请汇报：

(a) 附录有多少个 section / subsection？分别是什么内容？
(b) 哪些原本没有正文引用？现在加在了哪个章节哪一句话？
(c) 是否有附录内容被你判定为"无必要"而删除？删掉了哪些？

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WSojRxAFuXGeDh8Vo3LzLb`</summary>

```text
To isolate the depth signal from generator identity, we train a binary classifier (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) on each of the eight generators independently and evaluate on all others.
Table~\ref{tab:transfer} reports the full 8$\times$8 transfer matrix; Figure~\ref{fig:transfer-heatmap} (main text) visualizes it as a heatmap.
Note that these classifiers are trained with a binary objective, whereas the LOGO classifiers in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} are trained on a 3-class task and evaluated on the binary subtask; the two setups are therefore not directly comparable.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\scriptsize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\caption{Cross-generator binary F1 transfer matrix (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Each row trains on one generator; each column evaluates on another. Diagonal entries (\underline{underlined}) are within-generator. All 56 off-diagonal pairs exceed chance (0.5). Classifiers use the same 7-feature XGBoost configuration as LOGO but are trained with a binary objective.}
\label{tab:transfer}
===
```

```text
This appendix validates the causal predictive power of DDR features through three complementary analyses: permutation importance, Granger causality, and feature intervention.

\paragraph{Permutation Importance.}
Table~\ref{tab:perm-importance} reports the mean F1 drop when each feature's values are randomly shuffled across the test set (baseline F1\,=\,0.771).
Drifting features dominate: their average F1 drop (0.187) is $5.5\times$ larger than the near-stationary average (0.034), confirming that location-shift features, not shape features, drive depth classification.
===
\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption{Intra-family consistency of DDR patterns. Each cell shows the mean Spearman $|\rho|$(depth, feature) across generators within a family ($\pm$ SD for multi-generator families). Drifting features exhibit consistently high $|\rho|$ while near-stationary features remain low across all families. $H_{\mathrm{min}}$ is a \emph{boundary feature}: its elevated $|\rho| \approx 0.40$ reflects a large gen-0$\to$gen-1 transition, whereas its Cohen's $d \approx 0$ for gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ confirms near-stationarity within the binary classification scope (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).}
\label{tab:family_ddr_consistency}
\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{l c ccc c ccc}
\toprule

50	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textbf{Average (8-gen LOGO)}} & \textbf{0.694} & [.688, .700] & \textbf{0.762} \\

55	
56	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
57	\label{sec:results-differential}
58	
59	\paragraph{Formal definition.}
60	\looseness=-1 For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \in \{0,1,2{+}\}$, we define its \emph{depth sensitivity} as $\text{DDR}(f) = |\rho(\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.
61	A feature is classified as \emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; the two-cluster structure is robust across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$ ($k$-means silhouette\,=\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$; exhaustive split enumeration ranks this partition first; independent replication in all eight generators; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
62	\looseness=-1 Recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical~\citep{markovian_chains}, predicting location drift with shape invariance (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
63	
64	\looseness=-1 Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals the dichotomy: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat (per-feature distributions: Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}; qualitative chain example: Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative}).
65	The partition is binary ($k{=}2$; silhouette\,=\,0.660), with entropy std as the sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.46$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
70	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Drifting features (red; $|\rho| > 0.60$, effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) shift monotonically with generation depth; near-stationary features (blue; $d \leq 0.10$) are approximately preserved; entropy std (purple; $d{=}0.46$) is intermediate. Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values.}
71	\label{fig:feature-drift}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	\looseness=-1 Pooled Spearman correlations corroborate the split: drifting features show $|\rho| > 0.60$ (hapax ratio $-0.64$, TTR $-0.62$, entropy mean $-0.60$) vs.\ $|\rho| \leq 0.16$ for entropy max and tail decay; entropy min reaches $|\rho|{=}0.40$ due to its gen-0/gen-1 boundary sensitivity (discussed below)---a persistent magnitude gap defining the DDR partition (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}).
75	
76	\paragraph{Quantification.}
77	\looseness=-1 Cohen's $d$ shows a ${\geq}8{\times}$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) and near-stationary ($d < 0.10$); KS, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$ corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
78	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1/gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1; tail decay passes two one-sided tests (TOST) equivalence (Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
79	\paragraph{Validation.}
80	\looseness=-1 Each hold-out generator independently recovers the identical partition (silhouette 0.505--0.760); TOST confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8) and entropy max (6/8); permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$). Per-feature convergence dynamics (Figure~\ref{fig:convergence}) and per-family DDR consistency appear in Appendices~\ref{app:ddr-validation}--\ref{app:convergence}.
81	
82	\begin{figure}[t]
83	\centering
84	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_convergence_dynamics.pdf}
85	\caption{Feature convergence dynamics. Drifting features (red): $|d| > 1$ by gen-2+; near-stationary features (blue): $|d| < 0.25$ at all depths, confirming shape stability persists across chain lengths.}
86	\label{fig:convergence}
87	\end{figure}
88	
89	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
90	\looseness=-1 The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified cross-validation) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
91	Group ablation confirms complementarity: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only 0.697 vs.\ all-7 0.700)---shape stability contributes to depth \emph{grading}, not detection.
92	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
93	
94	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
95	\looseness=-1 Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) yield F1 spread of only 0.013 (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}); same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.2\,pp, but the DDR partition is preserved.
96	
97	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
98	\looseness=-1 All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
99	The DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), including cross-temperature transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-temp}); random word replacement at 10\% degrades binary F1 by only 8\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
100	
101	
102	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
103	\label{sec:results-baselines}
104

109	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
110	\end{figure}

      "content": "\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textbf{Average (8-gen LOGO)}} & \\textbf{0.694} & [.688, .700] & \\textbf{0.762} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textbf{LOFO (family-level)}} & 0.686 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Differential Depth Response}\n\\label{sec:results-differential}\n\n\\paragraph{Formal definition.}\n\\looseness=-1 For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \\in \\{0,1,2{+}\\}$, we define its \\emph{depth sensitivity} as $\\text{DDR}(f) = |\\rho(\\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.\nA feature is classified as \\emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \\emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; the two-cluster structure is robust across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$ ($k$-means silhouette\\,=\\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$; exhaustive split enumeration ranks this partition first; independent replication in all eight generators; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\\looseness=-1 Recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical~\\citep{markovian_chains}, predicting location drift with shape invariance (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).\n\n\\looseness=-1 Figure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals the dichotomy: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat (per-feature distributions: Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-distributions}; qualitative chain example: Appendix~\\ref{app:qualitative}).\nThe partition is binary ($k{=}2$; silhouette\\,=\\,0.660), with entropy std as the sole \\emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.46$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Drifting features (red; $|\\rho| > 0.60$, effect size $d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) shift monotonically with generation depth; near-stationary features (blue; $d \\leq 0.10$) are approximately preserved; entropy std (purple; $d{=}0.46$) is intermediate. Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values.}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\looseness=-1 Pooled Spearman correlations corroborate the split: drifting features show $|\\rho| > 0.60$ (hapax ratio $-0.64$, TTR $-0.62$, entropy mean $-0.60$) vs.\\ $|\\rho| \\leq 0.16$ for entropy max and tail decay; entropy min reaches $|\\rho|{=}0.40$ due to its gen-0/gen-1 boundary sensitivity (discussed below)---a persistent magnitude gap defining the DDR partition (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}).\n\n\\paragraph{Quantification.}\n\\looseness=-1 Cohen's $d$ shows a ${\\geq}8{\\times}$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.74$--$1.05$) and near-stationary ($d < 0.10$); KS, Glass's $\\Delta$, Cliff's $\\delta$ corroborate (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nEntropy min and tail decay are \\emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1/gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1; tail decay passes two one-sided tests (TOST) equivalence (Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\n\\looseness=-1 Each hold-out generator independently recovers the identical partition (silhouette 0.505--0.760); TOST confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8) and entropy max (6/8); permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$). Per-feature convergence dynamics (Figure~\\ref{fig:convergence}) and per-family DDR consistency appear in Appendices~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}--\\ref{app:convergence}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_convergence_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature convergence dynamics. Drifting features (red): $|d| > 1$ by gen-2+; near-stationary features (blue): $|d| < 0.25$ at all depths, confirming shape stability persists across chain lengths.}\n\\label{fig:convergence}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\n\\looseness=-1 The full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773, 10-fold stratified cross-validation) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation-table}).\nGroup ablation confirms complementarity: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only 0.697 vs.\\ all-7 0.700)---shape stability contributes to depth \\emph{grading}, not detection.\nTTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\n\\looseness=-1 Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) yield F1 spread of only 0.013 (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}); same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.2\\,pp, but the DDR partition is preserved.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}\n\\looseness=-1 All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\\,=\\,0.781; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nThe DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}), including cross-temperature transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-temp}); random word replacement at 10\\% degrades binary F1 by only 8\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\\ inference cost (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M model) matches multi-detector ensembles (1.4B total) at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost. RTX 5090 timings.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled). 7-feature: one 410M model; 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B. QLoRA (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}) uses full token sequences (within-model 5-fold CV, not LOGO)---an information-asymmetric ceiling.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule",

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_transfer_heatmap.pdf}
7	\caption{Cross-generator $8{\times}8$ transfer matrix (binary F1, gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Mean cross-generator F1\,=\,0.810 [95\% CI: 0.796, 0.822]; uniformly high off-diagonal values confirm depth signal transfers across five families without calibration.}
8	\label{fig:transfer-heatmap}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
12	\label{sec:results-classification}
13	
14	\looseness=-1 The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
15	This 3-class metric is inflated by shared gen-0 seeds (per-class gen-0 F1\,$\approx$\,0.94; \S\ref{sec:generation}); we therefore report \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) throughout, excluding gen-0 entirely.
16	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B; std.\ dev.\ 0.028), with the primary difficulty at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
17	
18	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
19	\looseness=-1 Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B; generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,1{,}000).}
20	Single-generator transfer confirms the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact (mean transfer F1\,=\,0.810, Figure~\ref{fig:transfer-heatmap}; LOFO\,=\,0.686); analysis of variance (ANOVA) confirms generator identity contributes ${<}1$\% of feature variance ($\eta^2_{\text{gen}} \leq 0.009$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).
21	A scale effect emerges: smaller models achieve higher F1 (Pythia-2.8B: 0.724 vs.\ Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
22	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; $+$10.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming additional depth signal in token-level representations; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (within-model; Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition as DDR's primary contribution.
23	
24	\paragraph{Confound controls.}
25	\looseness=-1 Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770; Appendix~\ref{app:length}); independent-seed evaluation with disjoint pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) confirms the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
26	
27	\paragraph{Error analysis.}
28	\looseness=-1 The gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary is the primary failure point: depth-2 samples---the shallowest within the 2+ class---exhibit the highest error rate (48.4\%), decreasing monotonically to 20.3\% at depth-4 (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}), reflecting insufficient accumulated drift beyond the large gen-0$\to$gen-1 distributional shift.
29	Error rates decrease monotonically at deeper generations ($\leq$29.2\% at gen-3+), confirming that DDR signal strengthens with chain length; the DDR decomposition is the primary analytical contribution.
30

LOFO\\,=\\,0.686); analysis of variance (ANOVA) confirms generator identity contributes ${<}1$\\% of feature variance ($\\eta^2_{\\text{gen}} \\leq 0.009$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-detail}).\nA scale effect emerges: smaller models achieve higher F1 (Pythia-2.8B: 0.724 vs.\\ Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (spread $\\leq$0.013; \\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nAn end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (within-model 5-fold CV; $+$10.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming additional depth signal in token-level representations; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 (within-model; Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the decomposition as DDR's primary contribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound controls.}\n\\looseness=-1 Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.724 (vs.\\ 0.770; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}); independent-seed evaluation with disjoint pools (F1\\,=\\,0.680; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-control}) confirms the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.\n\n\\paragraph{Error analysis.}\n\\looseness=-1 The gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+ boundary is the primary failure point: depth-2 samples---the shallowest within the 2+ class---exhibit the highest error rate (48.4\\%), decreasing monotonically to 20.3\\% at depth-4 (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}), reflecting insufficient accumulated drift beyond the large gen-0$\\to$gen-1 distributional shift.\nError rates decrease monotonically at deeper generations ($\\leq$29.2\\% at gen-3+), confirming that DDR signal strengthens with chain length; the DDR decomposition is the primary analytical contribution.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{8-generator LOGO macro-F1 (bootstrap 95\\% CIs, $n$\\,=\\,1{,}000). Binary F1 excludes gen-0. $^{\\dagger}$3-class F1 has upward bias from shared gen-0 seeds; independent-seed controls confirm the signal (F1\\,=\\,0.680; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-control}).}\n\\label{tab:logo-results}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Params} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{3-class F1}$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & \\textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] & 0.798 \\\\\nPythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.718 & [.700, .735] & \\textbf{0.806} \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.709 & [.693, .726] & 0.772 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B   & Llama   & 3.2B & 0.697 & [.680, .713] & 0.775 \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & 0.781 \\\\\nQwen3-8B       & Qwen    & 8.2B & 0.692 & [.674, .708] & 0.714 \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 0.6B & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & 0.780 \\\\\nMistral-7B     & Mistral & 7.0B & 0.633 & [.617, .648] & 0.682 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textbf{Average (8-gen LOGO)}} & \\textbf{0.694} & [.688, .700] & \\textbf{0.762} \\\\",
